zqnotesqz

Research notes

The 83 studies and books the chapters mention, chapter by chapter, each with what it found in plain words and where to read it. Each entry was looked up and checked: most against the paper itself or its abstract, some (mostly older books) against a publisher’s or library’s record. Unchecked works are left out. The full list, with notes on how strong each finding is, is at spreadloveandacceptance.com/research.

Reading research: a few words in plain English

The chapters often say how sure the research is: “a small study,” “linked with,” “didn’t replicate.” Here is what those words mean, so every note in the book is something you can weigh for yourself.

Correlational (“linked with”)
Two things tend to go together: people who sleep less tend to argue more. It doesn’t show which causes which, or whether a third thing, like a new baby, causes both.
Experiment
Researchers change one thing on purpose, ideally deciding by chance who gets it, and compare. Better at showing cause, though often in a lab, for a short time, with a small group.
Meta-analysis
A study of studies: it pools many studies of the same question. Usually more trustworthy than any one study, but only as good as the studies in it.
Replication
Doing a study again to see if the result holds. When a large team re-ran 100 published psychology studies in 2015, only about a third gave a clear result again, and the effects were on average about half as big. So one study is a start, not the end.
Effect size
How big a difference is, not just whether there is one. A result can be real (“significant”) and still tiny. The book says “small” or “modest” when that is the case.
Who was studied
Much of psychology studies students in Western, educated, well-off countries. A finding may not fit every age, culture or kind of family, and many studies of couples looked only at a mother and a father.
Self-report
People answered questions about themselves. That is often the only way to ask about feelings, but memory and wanting to look good both creep in.

How the book labels things: a Research note describes published findings in plain words and says where the evidence is thin. This site’s idea marks the book’s own ways of putting things into practice, such as the worksheets and the setup score, which have not been tested in studies. The full list at spreadloveandacceptance.com/research also says how strong each finding is: strong, moderate or early.

Preface to Part Two The work nobody sees

  • Allen, S. M., & Hawkins, A. J. (1999). Maternal gatekeeping: Mothers’ beliefs and behaviors that inhibit greater father involvement in family work. Journal of Marriage and the Family, 61(1), 199–212. Defined maternal gatekeeping along three dimensions and, in 622 dual-earner mothers, classified about one in five as gatekeepers. They did more family work each week and had less equal divisions of labor. doi.org/10.2307/353894
  • Clark, M. S., & Mills, J. (1979). Interpersonal attraction in exchange and communal relationships. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 37(1), 12–24. Distinguished communal relationships (benefits given in response to needs) from exchange relationships (benefits given in return for benefits). When people wanted a communal relationship, being repaid in kind reduced attraction. clarkrelationshiplab.yale.edu
  • Cowan, C. P., & Cowan, P. A. (1992). When Partners Become Parents: The Big Life Change for Couples. BasicBooks (New York); reissued 1999 (Erlbaum) and 2011. Based on the Becoming a Family Project, a long-term study of expectant couples, describes how strain and conflict between partners commonly rise across the transition to parenthood, and tests couples groups as prevention. openlibrary.org/works/OL92708W
  • Daminger, A. (2019). The cognitive dimension of household labor. American Sociological Review, 84(4), 609–633. Interviews with 35 couples (70 interviews) identified four parts of household “cognitive labor”: anticipating needs, identifying options, deciding, and monitoring. Women did more of it overall, especially anticipating and monitoring. Deciding was shared more equally. The work was often unseen by both partners. doi.org/10.1177/0003122419859007
  • Darley, J. M., & Latané, B. (1968). Bystander intervention in emergencies: Diffusion of responsibility. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 8(4), 377–383. Participants who believed others could also hear a person having a seizure were slower and less likely to help. The presence of others reduced each person’s sense of responsibility. doi.org/10.1037/h0025589
  • di Leonardo, M. (1987). The female world of cards and holidays: Women, families, and the work of kinship. Signs: Journal of Women in Culture and Society, 12(3), 440–453. Named “kin work”: the cards, calls, visits, gifts and gatherings that keep extended families connected. In the families she studied, it was done mostly by women and often treated as effortless. doi.org/10.1086/494338
  • Doss, B. D., Rhoades, G. K., Stanley, S. M., & Markman, H. J. (2009). The effect of the transition to parenthood on relationship quality: An 8-year prospective study. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 96(3), 601–619. Following 218 couples over the first eight years of marriage, those who had a first child showed sudden declines in relationship quality after the birth, on average, compared with couples who did not. doi.org/10.1037/a0013969
  • Gordon, A. M., Impett, E. A., Kogan, A., Oveis, C., & Keltner, D. (2012). To have and to hold: Gratitude promotes relationship maintenance in intimate bonds. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 103(2), 257–274. People who felt more appreciated by their romantic partners were more appreciative of their partners in return, and appreciation was linked with relationship maintenance and commitment. doi.org/10.1037/a0028723
  • Hochschild, A. R. (1983). The Managed Heart: Commercialization of Human Feeling. Berkeley: University of California Press (reissued 2003, 2012). Coined “emotional labor” from a study of flight attendants (and bill collectors) trained to display feelings at work. Distinguished surface acting from deep acting. search.worldcat.org/title/9280843
  • Hochschild, A. R., with Machung, A. (1989). The Second Shift: Working Parents and the Revolution at Home. Viking Penguin (updated edition 2012, Penguin Books). Interviews and home observations of dual-earner couples with young children found that working mothers did much more of the housework and childcare, adding up to a large extra load over a year. Many couples told themselves stories that the split was fair. penguinrandomhouse.com
  • Kurdek, L. A. (2007). The allocation of household labor by partners in gay and lesbian couples. Journal of Family Issues, 28(1), 132–148. Among 43 gay and 36 lesbian couples where both partners worked full-time, household tasks were divided more evenly than is typical in heterosexual couples, often by negotiation and specialization rather than gender. doi.org/10.1177/0192513X06292019
  • Ross, L., & Ward, A. (1996). Naive realism in everyday life: Implications for social conflict and misunderstanding. In E. S. Reed, E. Turiel, & T. Brown (Eds.), Values and Knowledge (pp. 103–135). Lawrence Erlbaum / Psychology Press. Described naive realism: people assume they see the world objectively, so those who disagree must be uninformed, irrational or biased, a dynamic that fuels conflict and misunderstanding. doi.org/10.4324/9780203773772-6
  • Ross, M., & Sicoly, F. (1979). Egocentric biases in availability and attribution. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 37(3), 322–336. In five experiments (married couples, groups, basketball teams), people recalled more of their own contributions to joint work and took more responsibility than others gave them. Spouses’ estimates of their own shares typically summed to more than 100 percent. doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.37.3.322
  • Schoppe-Sullivan, S. J., Brown, G. L., Cannon, E. A., Mangelsdorf, S. C., & Sokolowski, M. S. (2008). Maternal gatekeeping, coparenting quality, and fathering behavior in families with infants. Journal of Family Psychology, 22(3), 389–398. In 97 couples with infants, fathers were more involved in day-to-day care when mothers actively encouraged them (gate-opening). When mothers were highly critical (gate-closing), fathers’ own beliefs about involvement mattered less. doi.org/10.1037/0893-3200.22.3.389
  • Yavorsky, J. E., Kamp Dush, C. M., & Schoppe-Sullivan, S. J. (2015). The production of inequality: The gender division of labor across the transition to parenthood. Journal of Marriage and Family, 77(3), 662–679. Time-diary data from dual-earner couples showed a gender gap in total work opening up after a first baby: mothers took on most of the added childcare without cutting their paid hours. Survey reports overstated how equal things were. doi.org/10.1111/jomf.12189

Part Two · Chapter I Why we get out of tune

  • Bryant, C. M., Conger, R. D., & Meehan, J. M. (2001). The influence of in-laws on change in marital success. Journal of Marriage and Family, 63(3), 614–626. Following couples for several years, found that conflict and poorer relationships with in-laws predicted later declines in marital success (stability, satisfaction and commitment). doi.org/10.1111/j.1741-3737.2001.00614.x
  • Carrère, S., & Gottman, J. M. (1999). Predicting divorce among newlyweds from the first three minutes of a marital conflict discussion. Family Process, 38(3), 293–301. In 124 newlywed couples, how the first three minutes of a conflict discussion went (more negative and less positive emotion) predicted which couples divorced over the next six years. doi.org/10.1111/j.1545-5300.1999.00293.x
  • Gilovich, T., Savitsky, K., & Medvec, V. H. (1998). The illusion of transparency: Biased assessments of others' ability to read one's emotional states. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 75(2), 332–346. In several sets of studies, people overestimated how well others could read their internal states (for example, liars overestimated how detectable their lies were, and people thought their disgust showed more than it did). doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.75.2.332
  • Gordon, T. (1970). P.E.T.: Parent Effectiveness Training. New York: P. H. Wyden (book; revised 2000). A parenting book and course that popularized “I-messages”: describing your own feelings and the effect of a behavior rather than blaming the other person. search.worldcat.org/title/670595
  • Gottman, J. M. (1994). What predicts divorce? The relationship between marital processes and marital outcomes. Lawrence Erlbaum Associates (Hillsdale, NJ); reissued by Routledge. Gottman’s report of his long-term studies of couples, including “flooding”: feeling so physically overwhelmed in a conflict that listening and thinking become very hard. routledge.com
  • Kruger, J., Epley, N., Parker, J., & Ng, Z.-W. (2005). Egocentrism over e-mail: Can we communicate as well as we think? Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 89(6), 925–936. Across five experiments, people overestimated how well email recipients would detect their tone (such as sarcasm or seriousness), partly because they found it hard to step outside their own perspective. doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.89.6.925
  • Lally, P., van Jaarsveld, C. H. M., Potts, H. W. W., & Wardle, J. (2010). How are habits formed: Modelling habit formation in the real world. European Journal of Social Psychology, 40(6), 998–1009. Following 96 volunteers who repeated a daily behavior in the same context for 12 weeks, automaticity rose along a curve that leveled off. Reaching 95% of each person's plateau took between 18 and 254 days, and missing a single day did not meaningfully set progress back. doi.org/10.1002/ejsp.674
  • Lambert, N. M., Fincham, F. D., Stillman, T. F., Graham, S. M., & Beach, S. R. H. (2010). Motivating change in relationships: Can prayer increase forgiveness? Psychological Science, 21(1), 126–132. In two small experiments with students, people asked to pray for a romantic partner (study 1) or for a friend daily for four weeks (study 2) reported more willingness to forgive that person than people in comparison conditions. In study 2 this went with a rise in selfless concern. doi.org/10.1177/0956797609355634
  • Lieberman, M. D., Eisenberger, N. I., Crockett, M. J., Tom, S. M., Pfeifer, J. H., & Way, B. M. (2007). Putting feelings into words: Affect labeling disrupts amygdala activity in response to affective stimuli. Psychological Science, 18(5), 421–428. In a brain-imaging study, labeling the emotion in a picture reduced amygdala and other limbic responses compared with other ways of processing the image. doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-9280.2007.01916.x
  • Mahoney, A. (2010). Religion in families, 1999–2009: A relational spirituality framework. Journal of Marriage and Family, 72(4), 805–827. Reviewed a decade of peer-reviewed studies on religion in marriage and parent–child relationships, “for better and worse,” organizing them by how faith may help form, maintain and change family relationships. Faith can strengthen family life and can also be a source of strain. doi.org/10.1111/j.1741-3737.2010.00732.x
  • Milton, D. E. M. (2012). On the ontological status of autism: The “double empathy problem”. Disability & Society, 27(6), 883–887. A short conceptual paper arguing that misunderstanding between autistic and non-autistic people runs both ways: each side finds the other hard to read, so the problem lies between them, not only in the autistic person. autism.org.uk/advice-and-guidance/professional-practice/double-empathy
  • Rosenberg, M. B. (1999). Nonviolent Communication: A Language of Compassion (later editions: A Language of Life). PuddleDancer Press (Encinitas, CA); 2nd ed. 2003, 3rd ed. 2015. Describes Nonviolent Communication: expressing observations, feelings, needs and requests, and listening for the same in others. vufind.lboro.ac.uk/Record/562413
  • Ross, L. (1977). The intuitive psychologist and his shortcomings: Distortions in the attribution process. Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, 10, 173–220. Reviewed the biases of the lay 'intuitive psychologist' and named the fundamental attribution error: underestimating situational causes and overestimating personal dispositions when explaining others' behavior. doi.org/10.1016/S0065-2601(08)60357-3
  • Savitsky, K., Keysar, B., Epley, N., Carter, T., & Swanson, A. (2011). The closeness-communication bias: Increased egocentrism among friends versus strangers. Journal of Experimental Social Psychology, 47(1), 269–273. In three experiments, people overestimated how well they communicated with friends and spouses, more than with strangers, and made more egocentric errors following a friend's directions. Closeness seemed to relax the checking people do with strangers. sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0022103110002118
  • Yoo, S.-S., Gujar, N., Hu, P., Jolesz, F. A., & Walker, M. P. (2007). The human emotional brain without sleep—a prefrontal amygdala disconnect. Current Biology, 17(20), R877–R878. After a night without sleep, the amygdala reacted more strongly to negative pictures and was less connected to the prefrontal cortex. doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2007.08.007

Part Two · Chapter II Is the split working?

  • Cohen, S., Kamarck, T., & Mermelstein, R. (1983). A global measure of perceived stress. Journal of Health and Social Behavior, 24(4), 385–396. Introduced the Perceived Stress Scale, a short questionnaire about how unpredictable, uncontrollable and overloaded your life has felt lately. Scores went with symptoms of depression and illness. doi.org/10.2307/2136404
  • Cowan, C. P., & Cowan, P. A. (1992). When Partners Become Parents: The Big Life Change for Couples. BasicBooks (New York); reissued 1999 (Erlbaum) and 2011. Based on the Becoming a Family Project, a long-term study of expectant couples, describes how strain and conflict between partners commonly rise across the transition to parenthood, and tests couples groups as prevention. openlibrary.org/works/OL92708W
  • Daminger, A. (2019). The cognitive dimension of household labor. American Sociological Review, 84(4), 609–633. Interviews with 35 couples (70 interviews) identified four parts of household “cognitive labor”: anticipating needs, identifying options, deciding, and monitoring. Women did more of it overall, especially anticipating and monitoring. Deciding was shared more equally. The work was often unseen by both partners. doi.org/10.1177/0003122419859007
  • Darley, J. M., & Latané, B. (1968). Bystander intervention in emergencies: Diffusion of responsibility. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 8(4), 377–383. Participants who believed others could also hear a person having a seizure were slower and less likely to help. The presence of others reduced each person’s sense of responsibility. doi.org/10.1037/h0025589
  • di Leonardo, M. (1987). The female world of cards and holidays: Women, families, and the work of kinship. Signs: Journal of Women in Culture and Society, 12(3), 440–453. Named “kin work”: the cards, calls, visits, gifts and gatherings that keep extended families connected. In the families she studied, it was done mostly by women and often treated as effortless. doi.org/10.1086/494338
  • Doss, B. D., Rhoades, G. K., Stanley, S. M., & Markman, H. J. (2009). The effect of the transition to parenthood on relationship quality: An 8-year prospective study. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 96(3), 601–619. Following 218 couples over the first eight years of marriage, those who had a first child showed sudden declines in relationship quality after the birth, on average, compared with couples who did not. doi.org/10.1037/a0013969
  • Feinberg, M. E., & Kan, M. L. (2008). Establishing Family Foundations: Intervention effects on coparenting, parent/infant well-being, and parent-child relations. Journal of Family Psychology, 22(2), 253–263. A randomized trial with 169 expectant first-time couples found that the Family Foundations classes, delivered before and after birth, improved coparenting and some aspects of parent and infant wellbeing at follow-up. doi.org/10.1037/0893-3200.22.2.253
  • Goodhart, C. A. E. (1975). Problems of monetary management: The U.K. experience. Paper presented at the Conference in Monetary Economics, Sydney, July 1975; published in Papers in Monetary Economics, Vol. I (Reserve Bank of Australia, 1976); reprinted in Goodhart, Monetary Theory and Practice (Macmillan, 1984). The original source of “Goodhart’s law”: a statistical regularity tends to break down once authorities put pressure on it for control purposes. rba.gov.au/publications/rdp/1990/9013/conference-volumes.html
  • Hochschild, A. R., with Machung, A. (1989). The Second Shift: Working Parents and the Revolution at Home. Viking Penguin (updated edition 2012, Penguin Books). Interviews and home observations of dual-earner couples with young children found that working mothers did much more of the housework and childcare, adding up to a large extra load over a year. Many couples told themselves stories that the split was fair. penguinrandomhouse.com
  • Kurdek, L. A. (2007). The allocation of household labor by partners in gay and lesbian couples. Journal of Family Issues, 28(1), 132–148. Among 43 gay and 36 lesbian couples where both partners worked full-time, household tasks were divided more evenly than is typical in heterosexual couples, often by negotiation and specialization rather than gender. doi.org/10.1177/0192513X06292019
  • Ross, M., & Sicoly, F. (1979). Egocentric biases in availability and attribution. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 37(3), 322–336. In five experiments (married couples, groups, basketball teams), people recalled more of their own contributions to joint work and took more responsibility than others gave them. Spouses’ estimates of their own shares typically summed to more than 100 percent. doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.37.3.322
  • Strathern, M. (1997). ‘Improving ratings’: Audit in the British University system. European Review, 5(3), 305–321. Anthropological commentary on the “audit explosion” in British universities. Widely credited as the source of the popular phrasing of Goodhart’s law (when a measure becomes a target, it stops being a good measure). cambridge.org
  • U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (annual, since 2003). American Time Use Survey (ATUS). Federal survey (conducted by the U.S. Census Bureau). The federal survey of how, where and with whom Americans spend their time, including unpaid housework and childcare. It has run every year since 2003. bls.gov/tus
  • Yavorsky, J. E., Kamp Dush, C. M., & Schoppe-Sullivan, S. J. (2015). The production of inequality: The gender division of labor across the transition to parenthood. Journal of Marriage and Family, 77(3), 662–679. Time-diary data from dual-earner couples showed a gender gap in total work opening up after a first baby: mothers took on most of the added childcare without cutting their paid hours. Survey reports overstated how equal things were. doi.org/10.1111/jomf.12189

Part Two · Chapter III Full tanks and different angles

  • Amato, P. R. (2001). Children of divorce in the 1990s: An update of the Amato and Keith (1991) meta-analysis. Journal of Family Psychology, 15(3), 355–370. Updated a meta-analysis comparing children of divorced and continuously married parents. Differences in wellbeing were small on average and persisted in studies from the 1990s. doi.org/10.1037/0893-3200.15.3.355
  • Cohen, S., Kamarck, T., & Mermelstein, R. (1983). A global measure of perceived stress. Journal of Health and Social Behavior, 24(4), 385–396. Introduced the Perceived Stress Scale, a short questionnaire about how unpredictable, uncontrollable and overloaded your life has felt lately. Scores went with symptoms of depression and illness. doi.org/10.2307/2136404
  • Emery, R. E. (1982). Interparental conflict and the children of discord and divorce. Psychological Bulletin, 92(2), 310–330. Reviewed evidence that conflict between parents, in married and divorced families, is linked to children’s problems, and that the amount and type of conflict matter. doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.92.2.310
  • Gottman, J. M. (1994). What predicts divorce? The relationship between marital processes and marital outcomes. Lawrence Erlbaum Associates (Hillsdale, NJ); reissued by Routledge. Gottman’s report of his long-term studies of couples, including “flooding”: feeling so physically overwhelmed in a conflict that listening and thinking become very hard. routledge.com
  • Hetherington, E. M., & Kelly, J. (2002). For Better or For Worse: Divorce Reconsidered. W. W. Norton. Drawing on long-term studies of about 1,400 families, argues that most children of divorce and remarriage turn out reasonably well, with outcomes varying widely. wwnorton.co.uk/books/9780393324136-for-better-or-for-worse
  • Jones, E. E., & Nisbett, R. E. (1971). The actor and the observer: Divergent perceptions of the causes of behavior. General Learning Press (short monograph). Proposed the actor-observer hypothesis: people tend to explain their own actions by the situation and others' actions by their dispositions. krimdok.uni-tuebingen.de/Record/1098932900
  • Maccoby, E. E., & Mnookin, R. H. (1992). Dividing the Child: Social and Legal Dilemmas of Custody. Harvard University Press. Following more than a thousand separating families for three years, described three patterns of co-parenting among families where children spent time in both homes: cooperative, conflicted and disengaged. Most parents avoided legal battles but many found it hard to cooperate. hls.harvard.edu
  • Neff, K. D. (2003). Self-compassion: An alternative conceptualization of a healthy attitude toward oneself. Self and Identity, 2(2), 85–101. Defined self-compassion as three parts: self-kindness instead of harsh judgment, common humanity instead of isolation, and mindfulness instead of over-identifying with painful feelings. doi.org/10.1080/15298860309032
  • Neff, L. A., & Karney, B. R. (2009). Stress and reactivity to daily relationship experiences: How stress hinders adaptive processes in marriage. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 97(3), 435–450. Spouses under more stress outside the relationship reacted more strongly to everyday negative experiences within it. doi.org/10.1037/a0015663
  • Papernow, P. L. (2013). Surviving and Thriving in Stepfamily Relationships: What Works and What Doesn’t. Routledge. Describes “stepfamily architecture” and five common challenges it creates, drawing on research and clinical experience. Closeness between stepfamily members typically builds slowly. routledge.com
  • Ross, L., & Ward, A. (1996). Naive realism in everyday life: Implications for social conflict and misunderstanding. In E. S. Reed, E. Turiel, & T. Brown (Eds.), Values and Knowledge (pp. 103–135). Lawrence Erlbaum / Psychology Press. Described naive realism: people assume they see the world objectively, so those who disagree must be uninformed, irrational or biased, a dynamic that fuels conflict and misunderstanding. doi.org/10.4324/9780203773772-6

Part Two · Chapter IV Two kinds of fair

  • Adams, J. S. (1965). Inequity in social exchange. In L. Berkowitz (Ed.), Advances in Experimental Social Psychology, Vol. 2, pp. 267–299. Academic Press. Founding statement of equity theory: people compare the ratio of what they put in to what they get out with someone else’s, and feel tension when the ratios differ. doi.org/10.1016/S0065-2601(08)60108-2
  • Allen, S. M., & Hawkins, A. J. (1999). Maternal gatekeeping: Mothers’ beliefs and behaviors that inhibit greater father involvement in family work. Journal of Marriage and the Family, 61(1), 199–212. Defined maternal gatekeeping along three dimensions and, in 622 dual-earner mothers, classified about one in five as gatekeepers. They did more family work each week and had less equal divisions of labor. doi.org/10.2307/353894
  • Darley, J. M., & Latané, B. (1968). Bystander intervention in emergencies: Diffusion of responsibility. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 8(4), 377–383. Participants who believed others could also hear a person having a seizure were slower and less likely to help. The presence of others reduced each person’s sense of responsibility. doi.org/10.1037/h0025589
  • Deming, W. E. (1986). Out of the Crisis. MIT Press (Center for Advanced Engineering Study), Cambridge, MA. Deming’s case for quality management, arguing that most problems come from the system people work in rather than from individual workers. eploes.lib2.uniwa.gr/Record/lib2_1%2F6426/Details?lng=el
  • Deutsch, M. (1975). Equity, equality, and need: What determines which value will be used as the basis of distributive justice? Journal of Social Issues, 31(3), 137–149. Argued that equity is only one of several justice principles, alongside equality and need, and proposed when groups favor each: equality in cooperative relationships, equity in economic ones, need in caring ones. data.gesis.org/gesiskg/resource/zis-Deutsch1975Equity
  • Dew, J., Britt, S., & Huston, S. (2012). Examining the relationship between financial issues and divorce. Family Relations, 61(4), 615–628. In US national survey data on about 4,500 couples, financial disagreements were the strongest of the disagreement types in predicting later divorce, for both wives and husbands. doi.org/10.1111/j.1741-3729.2012.00715.x
  • Gottman, J. M. (1994). What predicts divorce? The relationship between marital processes and marital outcomes. Lawrence Erlbaum Associates (Hillsdale, NJ); reissued by Routledge. Gottman’s report of his long-term studies of couples, including “flooding”: feeling so physically overwhelmed in a conflict that listening and thinking become very hard. routledge.com
  • Klontz, B., Britt, S. L., Mentzer, J., & Klontz, T. (2011). Money beliefs and financial behaviors: Development of the Klontz Money Script Inventory. Journal of Financial Therapy, 2(1), 1–22. In 422 respondents, beliefs about money clustered into four “money scripts” (money avoidance, money worship, money status and money vigilance), several of which were linked with income and net worth. doi.org/10.4148/jft.v2i1.451
  • Kruger, J., Epley, N., Parker, J., & Ng, Z.-W. (2005). Egocentrism over e-mail: Can we communicate as well as we think? Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 89(6), 925–936. Across five experiments, people overestimated how well email recipients would detect their tone (such as sarcasm or seriousness), partly because they found it hard to step outside their own perspective. doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.89.6.925
  • Lieberman, M. D., Eisenberger, N. I., Crockett, M. J., Tom, S. M., Pfeifer, J. H., & Way, B. M. (2007). Putting feelings into words: Affect labeling disrupts amygdala activity in response to affective stimuli. Psychological Science, 18(5), 421–428. In a brain-imaging study, labeling the emotion in a picture reduced amygdala and other limbic responses compared with other ways of processing the image. doi.org/10.1111/j.1467-9280.2007.01916.x
  • Papp, L. M., Cummings, E. M., & Goeke-Morey, M. C. (2009). For richer, for poorer: Money as a topic of marital conflict in the home. Family Relations, 58(1), 91–103. In home diary records of marital conflict, money was not the most frequent topic, but money conflicts were more pervasive, problematic and recurrent than other conflicts and more likely to stay unresolved. doi.org/10.1111/j.1741-3729.2008.00537.x
  • Rick, S. I., Small, D. A., & Finkel, E. J. (2011). Fatal (fiscal) attraction: Spendthrifts and tightwads in marriage. Journal of Marketing Research, 48(2), 228–237. Tightwads and spendthrifts tend to marry each other. The bigger the difference within a couple, the more conflict over money, which in turn predicts lower marital wellbeing. doi.org/10.1509/jmkr.48.2.228
  • Ross, M., & Sicoly, F. (1979). Egocentric biases in availability and attribution. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 37(3), 322–336. In five experiments (married couples, groups, basketball teams), people recalled more of their own contributions to joint work and took more responsibility than others gave them. Spouses’ estimates of their own shares typically summed to more than 100 percent. doi.org/10.1037/0022-3514.37.3.322
  • Schoppe-Sullivan, S. J., Brown, G. L., Cannon, E. A., Mangelsdorf, S. C., & Sokolowski, M. S. (2008). Maternal gatekeeping, coparenting quality, and fathering behavior in families with infants. Journal of Family Psychology, 22(3), 389–398. In 97 couples with infants, fathers were more involved in day-to-day care when mothers actively encouraged them (gate-opening). When mothers were highly critical (gate-closing), fathers’ own beliefs about involvement mattered less. doi.org/10.1037/0893-3200.22.3.389
  • Stone, D., Patton, B., & Heen, S. (1999). Difficult Conversations: How to Discuss What Matters Most. Viking (New York); 10th-anniversary edition, Penguin 2010. A practitioner book from the Harvard Negotiation Project describing three conversations inside every hard conversation (what happened, feelings, identity) and the gap between intent and impact. penguinrandomhouse.com
  • Taylor, M. J., McNicholas, C., Nicolay, C., Darzi, A., Bell, D., & Reed, J. E. (2014). Systematic review of the application of the plan–do–study–act method to improve quality in healthcare. BMJ Quality & Safety, 23(4), 290–298. Of 73 healthcare articles reporting the use of plan–do–study–act cycles, most did not report key features of the method, such as iterative cycles, small tests of change and real-time data. doi.org/10.1136/bmjqs-2013-001862
  • Thibaut, J., & Walker, L. (1975). Procedural Justice: A Psychological Analysis. Lawrence Erlbaum Associates (Hillsdale, NJ). Founded procedural-justice research: people’s judgments of whether a decision process is fair (especially having a voice) shape how satisfied they are with the outcome. law.virginia.edu/node/127096

Part Two · Chapter V The monthly look-back

  • Boss, P. (1999). Ambiguous loss: Learning to live with unresolved grief. Harvard University Press. Named "ambiguous loss": losses without clear closure, such as someone physically absent but psychologically present, or present but psychologically changed (as in dementia), and suggested ways to cope. bookshare.org/browse/book/2050897
  • Darley, J. M., & Latané, B. (1968). Bystander intervention in emergencies: Diffusion of responsibility. Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 8(4), 377–383. Participants who believed others could also hear a person having a seizure were slower and less likely to help. The presence of others reduced each person’s sense of responsibility. doi.org/10.1037/h0025589
  • Dekker, S. (2007). Just Culture: Balancing Safety and Accountability. Ashgate (2nd ed. 2012). Argues for a “just culture” that separates honest mistakes from reckless behavior, and that who draws the line matters as much as where it is drawn. relbib.de/Record/1613173431
  • Deming, W. E. (1986). Out of the Crisis. MIT Press (Center for Advanced Engineering Study), Cambridge, MA. Deming’s case for quality management, arguing that most problems come from the system people work in rather than from individual workers. eploes.lib2.uniwa.gr/Record/lib2_1%2F6426/Details?lng=el
  • Goodhart, C. A. E. (1975). Problems of monetary management: The U.K. experience. Paper presented at the Conference in Monetary Economics, Sydney, July 1975; published in Papers in Monetary Economics, Vol. I (Reserve Bank of Australia, 1976); reprinted in Goodhart, Monetary Theory and Practice (Macmillan, 1984). The original source of “Goodhart’s law”: a statistical regularity tends to break down once authorities put pressure on it for control purposes. rba.gov.au/publications/rdp/1990/9013/conference-volumes.html
  • Hayslip, B., Jr., & Kaminski, P. L. (2005). Grandparents raising their grandchildren: A review of the literature and suggestions for practice. The Gerontologist, 45(2), 262–269. Reviewed research on custodial grandparents: the costs and benefits of raising a grandchild, how varied these families are, their need for social support, parenting, and help at several levels. doi.org/10.1093/geront/45.2.262
  • Pillemer, K. (2020). Fault Lines: Fractured Families and How to Mend Them. Avery (Penguin Random House). Draws on the Cornell Reconciliation Project, including a national survey on estrangement and interviews, to show family estrangement is more common than people assume and that some people reconcile. search.worldcat.org/oclc/1145305518
  • Reason, J. (1990). Human Error. Cambridge University Press. A theory of human error that sorts slips, lapses and mistakes, and introduced the layered-defenses thinking behind the “Swiss cheese” model of accidents. doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139062367
  • Reason, J. (1997). Managing the Risks of Organizational Accidents. Ashgate (now Routledge). Describes how organisational accidents arise from latent conditions combining with front-line errors, and sets out ideas for a safety and “just” culture. routledge.com
  • Strathern, M. (1997). ‘Improving ratings’: Audit in the British University system. European Review, 5(3), 305–321. Anthropological commentary on the “audit explosion” in British universities. Widely credited as the source of the popular phrasing of Goodhart’s law (when a measure becomes a target, it stops being a good measure). cambridge.org
  • Tversky, A., & Kahneman, D. (1973). Availability: A heuristic for judging frequency and probability. Cognitive Psychology, 5(2), 207–232. People judge how common something is by how easily examples come to mind. That usually works, but vivid or recent examples make some things seem more common than they are. apps.dtic.mil/sti/pdfs/ADA045770.pdf

Spread Love & Acceptance · For two or more

A book for people who share a home, work or care: partners, family, roommates, co-parents and coworkers. Each chapter takes one idea, from the work nobody sees to a fair way to share it and a kind way to talk about it, and comes with a worksheet that puts it to use.

“See it fairly. Say it kindly.”

  • Two sizes of every chapter: short and full
  • Everyday scenes, words you could use and one small thing to try
  • Free tools and worksheets that stay on your device
  • For every kind of relationship, and for people on their own

Free to read at spreadloveandacceptance.com/book

by Christian · Spread Love & Acceptance

Last updated: October 2026. From spreadloveandacceptance.com. Free to read and share for personal and non-commercial teaching use, with credit.
